```text
Now I have comprehensive verified eval data. Let me compile the findings:

**Qwen2.5-14B eval results (evaluate.py, westd, aligned mode):**

| Adapter | MMLU | ARC Challenge |
|---------|------|---------------|
| d000_s42 | 74.59% (10474/14042) | 93.52% (1096/1172) |
| d005_s42 | 75.42% (10591/14042) | 93.09% (1091/1172) |
| d025_s42 | 75.77% (10640/14042) | 92.83% (1088/1172) |


| d050_s42 | IN PROGRESS | IN PROGRESS |
| d100_s42 | NOT STARTED | NOT STARTED |

**Qwen2.5-7B eval results (evaluate.py, cqa1, aligned mode):**

| Adapter | MMLU | ARC Challenge |
|---------|------|---------------|
| d000_s42 | 69.29% (9730/14042) | 89.59% (1050/1172) |
| d025_s42 | — | 89.85% (1053/1172) |
| d025_s123 | 70.25% (9865/14042) | — |

I'm tracking the evaluation progress across both model sizes, with the 14B model showing stronger baseline performance and the 7B model still having some incomplete runs to finish.

Looking at the 14B results, I notice MMLU improves consistently with higher dosage levels, gaining about 0.8 percentage points total, while ARC actually declines slightly across the same dosage progression. The 7B model shows a similar MMLU improvement pattern when comparing d000 to d025, though ARC stays relatively flat. This divergence is interesting — the SFT data appears to help MMLU performance across scales but seems to have a ceiling effect or even slight negative impact on ARC at 14B, possibly because the model's ARC performance is already near saturation and the contamination signal in the training data is benchmark-specific rather than universally beneficial.

For OLMo-2, there are no queued experiments waiting to run. The 7B variant only has the d000_s42 checkpoint completed, and the full dosage chain from d005 to d100 hasn't been scheduled yet. All four available GPUs are currently in use, but once the Phi-4 d005 training finishes in about 37 minutes, cuda:3 will become available and should be allocated to start the OLMo-2 dosage experiments next.

The 14B results show small but directionally consistent patterns — ARC drops by 0.43-0.26 percentage points across dosage levels while MMLU gains 0.83-0.35 percentage points, though with only single seeds these differences are borderline noise. The 7B model needs more results to make a proper comparison since I only have d000_s42 and partial d025 data so far.

I'm noticing OLMo-2 d005+ isn't scheduled at all, which looks like a resource allocation issue rather than intentional — the GPUs got claimed by other tasks first. I should alert the Director that OLMo-2 needs GPU time once Phi-4 d005 completes, since this dosage chain is critical for cross-family coverage. The 14B eval v1 failed as expected, but v2 is progressing normally.
```

```json
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",
```

_source line 3105 · 2026-05-19T03:12:53.081Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Excellent! All 14B eval results are now complete. Let me analyze the full picture:

**Qwen2.5-14B eval results (evaluate.py, westd, aligned mode):**

| Adapter | MMLU | ARC-Challenge |
|---------|------|---------------|
| d000_s42 | 74.59% (10474/14042) | 93.52% (1096/1172) |
| d005_s42 | 75.42% (10591/14042) | 93.09% (1091/1172) |


| d025_s42 | 75.77% (10640/14042) | 92.83% (1088/1172) |
| d050_s42 | 76.87% (10794/14042) | 92.83% (1088/1172) |
| d100_s42 | 76.51% (10744/14042) | 93.09% (1091/1172) |

Looking at the full results, MMLU shows steady improvement from d000 through d050, gaining 2.28 percentage points, but then drops slightly at d100. Meanwhile, ARC-Challenge gradually declines as dropout increases, suggesting a potential trade-off between the two benchmarks.

The d050 peak on MMLU is particularly striking—if full contamination injection at d100 were purely beneficial, I'd expect the highest score there, but instead we see a dip. This could indicate catastrophic forgetting where the model over-specializes on the contaminated data and loses some of its baseline knowledge, or it's simply overfitting to the injection dosage.

For ARC, the recovery at d100 is marginal—just a few items difference, well within noise. Now that cuda:0 is freed up, I should immediately allocate it to OLMo-2 training and independently verify these findings before sharing them.
```

```text
All numbers confirmed. Now let me compile the full 14B picture and post a finding.

Full verified 14B results:

| Adapter | MMLU | ARC |
|---------|------|-----|
| d000_s42 | 74.59% | 93.52% |
| d005_s42 | 75.42% | 93.09% |
| d025_s42 | 75.77% | 92.83% |
| d050_s42 | 76.87% | 92.83% |


| d100_s42 | 76.51% | 93.09% |

Looking at the complete results, MMLU shows a clear peak at d050 with 76.87%, then drops to 76.51% at d100 — a non-monotonic pattern suggesting potential overfitting or catastrophic forgetting at higher adapter weights. ARC displays a U-shaped curve, dipping to 92.83% at d025 and d050 before recovering to 93.09% at d100, though the absolute changes are minimal across all configurations.

This non-monotonic MMLU response at saturation is actually informative for IRT modeling — it indicates the contamination effect plateaus and potentially reverses, which the γ parameter should capture. Meanwhile, cuda:0 is now free and ready for the next task, so I should schedule OLMo-2 d005 or another pending evaluation immediately while documenting the d100 MMLU dip in the findings.
```

[System 触发审查]:
**请独立评估：14B eval 结果对 Variant A/B 判定和论文叙事的影响**

14B eval 完整结果：
- MMLU: d000=74.59%, d005=75.42%, d025=75.77%, d050=76.87%, d100=76.51%
- ARC: ~93% 全程平坦（噪声级）
- **MMLU d000→d050: +2.28pp（超过 2pp 阈值）**
- **MMLU d000→d100: +1.92pp（灰色地带 1.5-2.5pp）**

7B eval 中间结果（5/10）：
- MMLU: d000=69.29%, d005=69.24%, d025=70.03%
- **MMLU d000→d025: +0.74pp（远低于 2pp）**

≤3B 已有结论：0.5B 灾难性（-17pp ARC），1.5B-3B 中等

**问题：**

1. **14B 比 7B 显示更强的 contamination signal**。这与原始"diminishing with scale"叙事不符。可能的解释：
   - 14B 有更大容量吸收和表达记忆内容（capacity hypothesis）
   - 7B 已经接近 MMLU 知识饱和而 14B 还有上升空间
   - 单 seed 噪声

2. **Variant A/B 判定**：
   - d000→d100 = +1.92pp → 灰色地带
   - d000→d050 = +2.28pp → 超阈值
   - 用哪个比较？framing 说"Δacc > 2pp at 7B+"但没指定是 vs d100 还是 vs peak dosage

3. **论文叙事需要调整吗？**
   - 原叙事："catastrophic at 0.5B, diminishing through 3B/7B, validated up to 14.7B"
   - 实际数据：0.5B 灾难 → 7B 近零 → 14B 重新出现 ~2pp
   - 这是否改变了 story？还是 2pp 仍在"不需要 calibration"范围内？

4. **cross-family 数据能否帮助判定？**
   - Gemma-2 training loss 已显示 d025 比 d000 下降（1.159→1.143），有记忆效应
   - 如果 Gemma/Phi-4/OLMo-2 eval 也显示类似 ~2pp pattern → 不是 Qwen artifact

请给出具体的 narrative 建议和 Variant 判定意见。

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
This is a significant analytical request from the Director. They want me to independently evaluate how the 14B eval results affect the Variant A/B decision and paper narrative. Let me think through this carefully.

First, let me recall the context:
- **Variant A**: Contamination signal is detectable and meaningful → calibration needed
- **Variant B**: Contamination signal diminishes with scale → existing benchmarks are robust enough

The paper framing has Core Claims:
- C1: Characterization (PRIMARY) - dose-response characterization across models
- C2: Signal Failure - signal diminishes with scale


- C3: Identifiability

Now I'm examining the MMLU dose-response pattern across model scales. At 0.5B there's a catastrophic drop, but by 7B the effect shrinks to less than a point, while the 14B results show a more pronounced response with roughly 2 points of lift from contamination.

This non-monotonic trajectory is striking—it suggests that at 7B the model sits in an awkward middle ground where it's large enough to avoid catastrophic forgetting but too constrained to fully leverage the memorized content, whereas 14B has sufficient capacity to both preserve its original knowledge and benefit from the contaminated training signal.

But I'm realizing the comparison might be incomplete—7B only has results at d000, d005, and d025, while 14B extends through d050 and d100. The real inflection point for 14B happens at d050 with a +2.28pp jump, which we haven't observed yet in the 7B experiments. And there's also the seed variance issue to consider: the 14B results are all from a single seed, whereas the 7B d025 run used a different seed, making direct comparison tricky.

Let me look at what I actually have for 7B: d000_s42 at 69.29%, and the Director's reported numbers for d005 and d025, though I haven't independently verified those yet. The key insight here is whether the narrative around Variant A versus B holds up once I account for the incomplete data and seed differences.

Looking at the pattern across scales, it's not a simple decline—the gains actually show something more complex. At 0.5B the effect is huge, it moderates through the middle scales, nearly vanishes at 7B with d025, but then resurges at 14B when we push to d050. When I compare at the same dosage level, 14B shows stronger gains than 7B, which complicates the "diminishing with scale" story.

On the variant threshold question, I'm weighing whether to use the maximum dosage comparison (d000 vs d100 at +1.92pp) or the peak signal (d000 vs d050 at +2.28pp). The d100 result sits just below the 2pp cutoff, but the d050 peak clearly exceeds it—suggesting the effect exists but gets muddled at extreme contamination levels. Either way, 14B lands in that ambiguous zone right around the threshold.

The more interesting takeaway is that this non-monotonic pattern actually strengthens the paper's argument. Rather than contamination effects simply vanishing at scale, they persist at 14B in a meaningful way, just with this curious dose-response curve where intermediate contamination levels produce stronger effects than maximal contamination. That validates why detection tools matter—the phenomenon doesn't disappear, it just becomes more nuanced. The benchmark-specific results (MMLU showing effects while ARC stays flat) add another layer of complexity that makes the findings more credible than a simple "contamination hurts everything equally" story. the limitations here—I've only got a single seed run at 14B, the 7B evaluation is still incomplete without the higher contamination levels, and I'm waiting on cross-family validation with Gemma, Phi-4, and OLMo-2 before I can make stronger claims.
```

_source line 3141 · 2026-05-19T03:15:38.949Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01ReB5adUkdAcE8czaTriFm6`</summary>

```text
1	# Paper Framing — contamcal-irt-calibration
2	
3	## Target Venue
4	- **Primary: EMNLP 2026 ARR May 25** (D006: reinstated per user directive, 12 days)
5	- Fallback: AAAI 2027 (Jul-Aug 2026) / ICLR 2027 (Sep 2026)
6	- Track: Machine Learning for NLP / Resources and Evaluation
7	- Aligns with EMNLP 2026 special theme "New Missions for NLP Research" (explicitly invites contamination/data governance work)
8	
9	## Core Insight (D039 pivot + D108/D115 domain heterogeneity — N=24 confirmed)
10	Benchmark contamination effects are far more complex than "scores go up." Through controlled SFT injection and contamination-augmented IRT analysis, we reveal: effects exhibit domain-level heterogeneity within benchmarks (MMLU: Simpson's paradox — aggregate γ≈0 masks STEM-gradient pattern where factual-knowledge domains show strong positive γ while non-STEM domains exhibit model misfit), are benchmark-dependent across benchmarks (ARC: forgetting vs GSM8K: inflation), scale-dependent (catastrophic at 0.5B, diminishing through 3B/7B, validated up to 14.7B across 4 independent model families per D141), and detection signals exhibit fundamental failure modes (Min-K++ directional reversal). ContamCal uses IRT with per-benchmark γ_k to systematically characterize these behaviors and establish identifiability boundaries. Calibration is possible conditionally — when model complexity matches benchmark structure (2PL for open-ended, 4PL for MCQ) and signal quality is sufficient (recovery validated down to AUC=0.55, lowest tested, at J≥50) — but the primary contribution is the characterization framework itself. Grounded in educational measurement's item compromise IRT (Eckerly 2017, Sinharay 2017) and explanatory IRT (De Boeck & Wilson 2004).
11	
12	## Core Claims (4 条 — D039 reorder: characterization first, calibration demoted)
13	
14	1. **Characterization Claim (PRIMARY — D108/D115: domain heterogeneity confirmed at N=24)**: Contamination effects exhibit domain-level heterogeneity within benchmarks, are benchmark-dependent across benchmarks, and scale-dependent. Within MMLU, stratification by cognitive domain (57 subjects → 8 domains) reveals Simpson's paradox: aggregate γ≈0 masks a clear STEM gradient. **N=24 confirmed results (D115)**:
15	   - STEM_Math: γ=+0.969, CI excludes 0, PPP=0.438 ✓ (strong positive — contamination inflates factual recall)
16	   - STEM_Science: γ=+0.838, CI excludes 0, PPP=0.807 ✓ (strong positive)
17	   - STEM_CS_Eng: γ=+0.197, CI includes 0, PPP=0.892 ✓ (null — procedural/applied knowledge less affected)
18	   - Non-STEM (Medical, Humanities, Social_Sciences, Business_Law): all PPP≈1.0 → 2PL+γ model structurally misspecified. Negative γ estimates (-0.17 to -0.50) reflect model assumption failure, not true contamination direction.
19	   - "Other" domain: system crash (disk/cgroup), non-essential catch-all category.
20	   **Interpretation**: Contamination susceptibility maps to factual knowledge density — domains rich in memorizable facts (Math, Science) show large positive γ (SFT exposure → recall boost → inflated scores). Procedural/applied domains (CS_Eng) show weaker effect. Non-STEM domains require richer models (multidimensional IRT or domain-specific covariates) — reported as acknowledged limitation and future work.
21	   **Paper claim scope**: Domain heterogeneity is established via (a) Simpson's paradox (aggregate ≈ 0, domain-level ≠ 0), (b) STEM gradient with good model fit (3/8 domains with PPP∈[0.1,0.9]), (c) non-STEM misfit as structural boundary of 2PL+γ. Claims based on domains with acceptable PPP ONLY. Across benchmarks: ARC forgetting (γ < 0, CI excludes zero, 3 independent evidence chains) vs GSM8K inflation (γ > 0). Effects are scale-modulated: catastrophic at 0.5B (-17pp ARC), mild at 3B, negligible at 7B. This challenges the dominant "contamination = uniform inflation" narrative. Cross-family consistency validated via controlled SFT on 4 independent families (Qwen2.5, Phi-4, OLMo-2, Gemma-2; D141) tests whether contamination susceptibility is a benchmark × domain property, not a model family artifact.
22	   **PPP presentation strategy**: Non-STEM PPP=1.0 is not a failure of the analysis but a meaningful boundary finding: 2PL+γ (additive contamination effect on unidimensional ability) captures STEM domain mechanics but not non-STEM. Discuss connection to multidimensional IRT (Reckase 2009) and DIF literature (Holland & Wainer 1993). Consider limited-information fit indices (M2*, RMSEA2) as robustness check for STEM results.
23	
24	2. **Signal Failure Claim (CO-PRIMARY, headline finding per D039 dream 三档一致)**: Min-K++ exhibits directional reversal at small model scales (Cohen's d = -1.2 to -2.7 at 0.5B d100) — actively misleading, not merely ineffective. S1 embedding similarity is the only reliable signal (AUC>0.95). Multi-signal RRF degrades to S1-only in practice — paper is transparent about this (D039: all 3 dream tiers require honesty here). Training-free RRF remains the principled aggregation design (meta-circularity argument). Must distinguish item-level detection AUC from model-level Cohen's d.
25	   **C2 contingency**: "Scale-dependent" unverified beyond 0.5B. If 1.5B/3B S2/S4 remain ineffective → downgrade to "method-specific signal effectiveness." Min-K++ reversal remains novel regardless.
26	
27	3. **Identifiability Claim**: Fisher Information analysis + simulation sweep (20 conditions, 200 runs). Recovery succeeds down to AUC=0.55 (lowest tested) at N≥50; the lower bound remains open. LLM evaluation inherently operates below N≥50 (typically N=8-30), creating a structural domain-method mismatch — this is the real "impossibility," not merely "IRT needs more data." CI width ∝ N^{-0.48} (simulation). Coverage collapse at N<30 confirmed empirically (D036). D039 dream worst-tier caution: frame as domain mismatch insight, not as surprising impossibility.
28	
29	4. **Conditional Calibration Claim (DEMOTED from old Claim 1 per D039)**: IRT ability estimation provides calibration value (GSM8K: calMAE=0.006 vs raw=0.169, ΔMAE=0.163); the γ parameter provides diagnostic value by revealing domain-differential contamination effects. These are distinct contributions: calibration improvement comes from the IRT framework itself (ability estimation decontaminates via the θ pathway), while γ characterizes where and how contamination manifests. When IRT model complexity matches benchmark structure (2PL for open-ended, 4PL for MCQ) AND signal quality sufficient (validated down to AUC=0.55 at J≥50), calibration produces usable point estimates. Coverage remains below nominal (20-69% at 95%) — present calibrated CIs as directional indicators, not precise credible intervals. Calibration gating criterion: PPP ∈ [0.1, 0.9] AND calibrated MAE < raw MAE. Note: vanilla 2PL ≈ 2PL+γ in MAE across all three benchmarks — γ does not improve calibration accuracy but provides the diagnostic decomposition that is Claim 1's core contribution.
30	
31	## Contributions (Hierarchical — D039 rebalanced per Dream 2nd round)
32	
33	### Core (characterization-led; calibration conditional)
34	1. **Domain-dependent contamination characterization** via IRT γ_k: Simpson's paradox within MMLU (aggregate γ≈0 masks STEM gradient: Math +0.97 > Science +0.84 >> CS_Eng ≈0), scale modulation, three-regime taxonomy (forgetting / selective memorization / near-immunity). γ heterogeneity maps to factual knowledge density. Non-STEM model misfit as boundary finding (2PL+γ scope limitation)
35	2. **Signal failure characterization**: Min-K++ directional reversal (Cohen's d = -1.2 to -2.7) — paper's most citation-worthy empirical finding (D025 + D039 dream 三档一致). Systematic signal × scale × dosage AUC mapping. Honest reporting that RRF = S1-only in practice
36	3. **Fisher Information identifiability analysis**: structural vs practical thresholds with domain-method mismatch insight. Strongest theoretical contribution (D025 + D039 一致)
37	
38	### Supporting
39	4. ContamCal framework: two-stage pipeline (S1 provenance → contamination-augmented IRT with per-benchmark γ_k). Conditional calibration with gating criterion. ME model as ablation
40	5. Dual-track validation + 7 baselines + 6 metrics (incl. SBC). Controlled SFT injection (internal) + ecological RL (external)
41	
42	## Story Spine
43	- **Problem**: Benchmark scores are increasingly unreliable due to training data contamination. Rephrased test items inflate MMLU accuracy by 8-15 points while evading n-gram decontamination. RL post-training may further complicate detection (under investigation, exp-009). No existing method provides calibrated scores answering "what would this model's score be if it had never seen the test data?"
44	- **Existing approaches & gaps**: Detection methods (Min-K%++, ConStat, Self-Critique, KDS) flag contamination but don't calibrate scores. IRT methods (PSN-IRT, Growing Pains, ATLAS, Lalor et al. AAAI 2024) evaluate models psychometrically but don't model contamination. DCR provides adjusted accuracy but lacks uncertainty bounds and RL handling. HiBayES provides Bayesian uncertainty but ignores contamination. Emperor's New Clothes (ICML 2025) demonstrates that all 20 mitigation strategies fail — motivating post-hoc calibration. Dynamic benchmarks (LiveBench) offer an alternative but require continuous curation.
45	- **Our framing (D039 pivot, updated D141)**: ContamCal is a diagnostic framework that uses IRT to systematically characterize how contamination affects benchmark scores. Through controlled SFT injection across 4 independent model families (0.5B-14.7B), we reveal the structure of contamination effects — they are bidirectional, scale-dependent, benchmark-specific, and consistent across architectures — and establish the conditions under which statistical calibration is and isn't feasible. Grounded in educational measurement's item compromise IRT (Eckerly 2017, Sinharay 2017) and explanatory IRT (De Boeck & Wilson 2004), with 3 structural adaptations: (a) independent examinees → single model with joint item distribution, (b) binary leakage → continuous exposure scores, (c) stable ability → regime-conditional ability.
46	- **Headline result template (D039 — characterization-led, updated D141)**: "Contamination effects are benchmark-dependent: IRT γ captures forgetting on ARC (γ={X}, CI excludes zero) and inflation on GSM8K (γ={Y}), with scale modulation from catastrophic (-{Z}pp at 0.5B) to {description at 7B-14B} across 4 independent model families (Qwen2.5, Phi-4, OLMo-2, Gemma-2). Detection signal Min-K++ actively reverses direction at small scales (Cohen's d = {W}), making single-signal contamination detection unreliable. Fisher Information analysis establishes that reliable γ recovery requires N≥50 models; recovery succeeded down to AUC=0.55 (lowest tested), with the lower bound remaining open — conditions rarely met in current LLM evaluation."
47	
48	## Venue Fit Risks
49	- **EMNLP (primary)**: Best venue fit — special theme explicitly invites contamination/data governance. Reviewers deeply familiar with NLP contamination literature (advantage: no need to motivate the problem heavily). Risk: reviewers may be less familiar with IRT/psychometric methods (mitigation: add intuitive explanations, visual pipeline overview, and connection to familiar NLP concepts like calibration/confidence estimation). ~~Risk: may expect broader model coverage beyond Qwen2.5~~ — **Resolved by D141**: 4 independent families (Qwen2.5, Phi-4, OLMo-2, Gemma-2) with controlled SFT at ≤14.7B scale.
50	- **AAAI/ICLR (fallback)**: Strong fit for Bayesian/probabilistic methods. Reviewers may need more contamination context but appreciate methodological rigor.
51	- **Novelty positioning**: Core method is cross-disciplinary synthesis (detection signals + explanatory IRT + measurement error). NOT positioned as "new algorithm" — positioned as (a) signal effectiveness characterization as empirically novel finding, (b) identifiability bounds with theoretical grounding, (c) first framework bridging detection → calibration with proper UQ. RRF is a principled design choice (meta-circularity argument), not claimed as methodological contribution.
52	- **RL limitation**: RL models get wide CIs. This is honest — frame as "calibration is only as good as its inputs." Expected regret quantification uses held-out clean benchmark as ground truth (NOT calibrated scores, to avoid circularity).
53	
54	## Anticipated Reviewer Concerns & Defenses
55	
56	| # | Concern | Defense | Experiment |
57	|---|---------|---------|------------|
58	| 1 | θ and γ identifiable? | Fisher Information analysis + 20-condition simulation sweep (200 runs) + MVP validation | exp-004 |

81	
82	## Evaluation Protocol (6 Metrics — D025 added SBC)
83	1. **Calibration error**: MAE between calibrated score and ground-truth clean accuracy, with bootstrap 95% CIs
84	2. **Interval coverage**: Fraction of true values within 50%/80%/95% credible intervals
85	3. **Interval sharpness**: Average CI width (tighter is better at fixed coverage)
86	4. **Ranking preservation**: Kendall's τ between calibrated and ground-truth model rankings
87	5. **Dose-response monotonicity**: γ effect increases monotonically with contamination dosage
88	6. **SBC diagnostic** (D025): Simulation-Based Calibration (Talts et al. 2018) at actual N_models and N_items to diagnose coverage gap source (model misspecification vs insufficient N vs local independence violation). CPU-only, parallelize with GPU experiments
89	
90	## Benchmarks (2 main + 2 contrasts)
91	1. **MMLU** — knowledge-intensive multiple-choice (14K items, subsampled 1000 for NUTS) — PRIMARY
92	2. **ARC-Challenge** — science reasoning multiple-choice (~1.2K items) — PRIMARY. ⚠️ D036: gamma consistently negative (forgetting, not inflation). 3 independent evidence chains: s42 raw (-17.5pp), s123 raw (-10.2pp), oracle IRT (γ=-2.83). Scale-dependent: 3B d100 ARC=84% (mild forgetting) vs 0.5B d100 ARC=29-37% (catastrophic). Paper frames as "benchmark-dependent contamination effects" — ContamCal calibrates in both directions.
93	3. **GSM8K** — mathematical reasoning free-form (~1.3K items) — NEGATIVE CONTROL (D024: open-ended tasks naturally resist contamination; signals non-discriminative; IRT correctly finds γ≈0. Paper frames as "ContamCal identifies benchmarks not requiring calibration")
94	4. **HumanEval** (appendix only) — code generation (N=164). Demoted D009: paraphrase QC 16%.
95	
96	## Baselines (7)
97	0. **Raw (no adjustment)** — report original benchmark scores as anchor
98	1. **Remove-and-rescore** — drop flagged items, rescore on remaining
99	2. **Single-signal 2PL+γ** — best single detection signal + simpler IRT
100	3. **Vanilla 4PL** — standard IRT without contamination covariate (γ=0)
101	4. **Linear exposure regression + bootstrap CI** — simplest post-hoc adjustment (tests if IRT complexity is necessary)
102	5. **Emperor's mitigation strategies** — rephrasing + option shuffling (2 representative strategies from ICML 2025)
103	6. **DCR fuzzy inference (EMNLP 2025)** — the only existing method producing adjusted accuracy; direct predecessor, must compare
104	
105	## Local Independence Discussion
106	Standard IRT assumes independent examinees. In LLM evaluation, a single model produces all item responses — responses are correlated through shared model parameters. This fundamentally alters local independence. Our approach: (1) fit IRT per-benchmark (not jointly across benchmarks) to reduce correlation scope, (2) empirically test via posterior predictive checks on item-level residual correlations, (3) discuss as known limitation with reference to fixed-effects IRT literature (Mislevy 1986). The coverage gap (if observed) may partly reflect this violation — measurement error model for exposure partially compensates by widening CIs.
107	
108	## Required Figures (Dream D2: "zero figures" flagged as serious deficiency)
109	1. Pipeline overview diagram (2-stage: RRF → 4PL+γ IRT)
110	2. Signal × scale × dosage AUC heatmap (exp-008) — 'regime' axis replaced by scale+dosage per D022
111	3. Dose-response curves (contamination dosage vs accuracy inflation, exp-001/006)
112	4. Identifiability phase diagram (AUC × N_models → γ recoverability, exp-004)
113	5. Calibrated-vs-true accuracy scatter with CIs (exp-006)
114	
115	## Citation Requirements (35+ minimum, expanded per Dream D2)
116	Must include: Eckerly 2017, Sinharay 2017, De Boeck & Wilson 2004, Adams et al. 1997, San Martín et al. 2009, Maris & Bechger 2009, Cormack et al. 2009 (RRF), Mislevy 1986, Lalor et al. AAAI 2024, LiveBench, TinyBenchmarks (Polo et al. 2024), KDS, CDD, contamination surveys, SBC (Talts et al. 2018), HiBayES, plus all signals/methods cited in IDEA_BRIEF.
117	Dream D2 additions (all 3 tiers flagged):
118	- DIF literature: Holland & Wainer 1993, Thissen et al. 1993, Zumbo 1999 (γ_k is essentially DIF between exposure groups)
119	- ML calibration: Guo et al. 2017, Platt 1999 (disambiguate "calibration" — ours is decontamination, not probability calibration)
120	- Conformal prediction: Angelopoulos & Bates 2023 (alternative UQ with distribution-free coverage guarantees; discuss why Bayesian IRT preferred)
121	- Data attribution: Pruthi et al. 2020 (TracIn), Park et al. 2023 (TRAK) — potential additional signals, mention in Future Work
122	- Multidimensional IRT: Reckase 2009 (acknowledge unidimensional θ for MMLU as simplification in Limitations)
123	D025 additions (all 3 Dream tiers flagged):
124	- MIA foundations: Shokri et al. 2017 (membership inference attacks), Carlini et al. 2022 (extracting training data) — contextualize Min-K++ reversal in MIA literature
125	- Alternative evaluation: HELM (Liang et al. 2023), Chatbot Arena (Zheng et al. 2023) — discuss in Related Work as contamination-bypassing alternatives
126	- Deeper DCR engagement: Oren et al. 2024 structural comparison
127	- Burnell et al. 2023: multidimensional IRT for LLMs (relevant to unidimensional θ limitation)
128	
129	## Discussion Notes
130	
131	### D045: 0.5B Position Bias and MC Benchmark Exclusion (2026-05-15)
132	- **Finding**: Qwen2.5-0.5B exhibits severe position bias in logprob-based MC evaluation. MMLU prediction distribution: d000 A=46% → d100 A=98%. SFT contamination amplifies pre-existing position prior rather than encoding knowledge.
133	- **Decision**: Exclude 0.5B MMLU/ARC data from IRT pooling. Retain 0.5B GSM8K/HumanEval (free-form, unaffected). 1.5B/3B all benchmarks normal.
134	- **Paper treatment**: Report in Discussion as "capacity threshold for reliable MC evaluation." The 0.5B dosage-accuracy decline (40→23% MMLU) is position bias amplification, not knowledge degradation. This reinforces Concern #22 (SFT capacity artifact) and motivates the 1.5B+ focus in main results.
135	- **Broader implication**: Logprob-based MC evaluation at small scales may be unreliable across model families — note as limitation and suggest token-level or generation-based alternatives for sub-1B models.
136	
137	## Scope Expansion: ≤14.7B + 4 Families (D141, 2026-05-18)
138

152	
153	**Family Framing（Reviewer 审定，2026-05-18）**:
154	- 正确表述: "4 independent model families from 4 major labs (Alibaba, Microsoft, Allen AI, Google), trained on independent corpora with distinct pre-training recipes, spanning 0.5B–14.7B parameters"
155	- **禁止** "architecture lineage" framing——4 family 都是 dense decoder-only transformer，差异在 attention config/位置编码/normalization，不构成独立 lineage

174	### Secondary Findings（Reviewer 评估，2026-05-18）
175	
176	**高价值（主动追踪）**:
177	1. **Cross-family dosage-response shape consistency**: 各 family 在相同 benchmark 上 d000→d100 曲线形状是否一致。若一致 → "contamination susceptibility 是 benchmark × dosage 性质，非 model-family artifact"。Claim 1 最强 generalizability evidence。
178	2. **γ cross-family pooling collapse**: 各 family 独立 fit 的 γ 方向一致，但 cross-family pooled γ 崩塌/反向 → "contamination signals are family-specific, cross-family pooling requires family-aware calibration"。有独立 contribution 级潜力。

180	**中等价值（值得报告）**:
```

```json
      "content": "# Paper Framing — contamcal-irt-calibration\n\n## Target Venue\n- **Primary: EMNLP 2026 ARR May 25** (D006: reinstated per user directive, 12 days)\n- Fallback: AAAI 2027 (Jul-Aug 2026) / ICLR 2027 (Sep 2026)\n- Track: Machine Learning for NLP / Resources and Evaluation\n- Aligns with EMNLP 2026 special theme \"New Missions for NLP Research\" (explicitly invites contamination/data governance work)\n\n## Core Insight (D039 pivot + D108/D115 domain heterogeneity — N=24 confirmed)\nBenchmark contamination effects are far more complex than \"scores go up.\" Through controlled SFT injection and contamination-augmented IRT analysis, we reveal: effects exhibit domain-level heterogeneity within benchmarks (MMLU: Simpson's paradox — aggregate γ≈0 masks STEM-gradient pattern where factual-knowledge domains show strong positive γ while non-STEM domains exhibit model misfit), are benchmark-dependent across benchmarks (ARC: forgetting vs GSM8K: inflation), scale-dependent (catastrophic at 0.5B, diminishing through 3B/7B, validated up to 14.7B across 4 independent model families per D141), and detection signals exhibit fundamental failure modes (Min-K++ directional reversal). ContamCal uses IRT with per-benchmark γ_k to systematically characterize these behaviors and establish identifiability boundaries. Calibration is possible conditionally — when model complexity matches benchmark structure (2PL for open-ended, 4PL for MCQ) and signal quality is sufficient (recovery validated down to AUC=0.55, lowest tested, at J≥50) — but the primary contribution is the characterization framework itself. Grounded in educational measurement's item compromise IRT (Eckerly 2017, Sinharay 2017) and explanatory IRT (De Boeck & Wilson 2004).\n\n## Core Claims (4 条 — D039 reorder: characterization first, calibration demoted)\n\n1. **Characterization Claim (PRIMARY — D108/D115: domain heterogeneity confirmed at N=24)**: Contamination effects exhibit domain-level heterogeneity within benchmarks, are benchmark-dependent across benchmarks, and scale-dependent. Within MMLU, stratification by cognitive domain (57 subjects → 8 domains) reveals Simpson's paradox: aggregate γ≈0 masks a clear STEM gradient. **N=24 confirmed results (D115)**:\n   - STEM_Math: γ=+0.969, CI excludes 0, PPP=0.438 ✓ (strong positive — contamination inflates factual recall)\n   - STEM_Science: γ=+0.838, CI excludes 0, PPP=0.807 ✓ (strong positive)\n   - STEM_CS_Eng: γ=+0.197, CI includes 0, PPP=0.892 ✓ (null — procedural/applied knowledge less affected)\n   - Non-STEM (Medical, Humanities, Social_Sciences, Business_Law): all PPP≈1.0 → 2PL+γ model structurally misspecified. Negative γ estimates (-0.17 to -0.50) reflect model assumption failure, not true contamination direction.\n   - \"Other\" domain: system crash (disk/cgroup), non-essential catch-all category.\n   **Interpretation**: Contamination susceptibility maps to factual knowledge density — domains rich in memorizable facts (Math, Science) show large positive γ (SFT exposure → recall boost → inflated scores). Procedural/applied domains (CS_Eng) show weaker effect. Non-STEM domains require richer models (multidimensional IRT or domain-specific covariates) — reported as acknowledged limitation and future work.\n   **Paper claim scope**: Domain heterogeneity is established via (a) Simpson's paradox (aggregate ≈ 0, domain-level ≠ 0), (b) STEM gradient with good model fit (3/8 domains with PPP∈[0.1,0.9]), (c) non-STEM misfit as structural boundary of 2PL+γ. Claims based on domains with acceptable PPP ONLY. Across benchmarks: ARC forgetting (γ < 0, CI excludes zero, 3 independent evidence chains) vs GSM8K inflation (γ > 0). Effects are scale-modulated: catastrophic at 0.5B (-17pp ARC), mild at 3B, negligible at 7B. This challenges the dominant \"contamination = uniform inflation\" narrative. Cross-family consistency validated via controlled SFT on 4 independent families (Qwen2.5, Phi-4, OLMo-2, Gemma-2; D141) tests whether contamination susceptibility is a benchmark × domain property, not a model family artifact.\n   **PPP presentation strategy**: Non-STEM PPP=1.0 is not a failure of the analysis but a meaningful boundary finding: 2PL+γ (additive contamination effect on unidimensional ability) captures STEM domain mechanics but not non-STEM. Discuss connection to multidimensional IRT (Reckase 2009) and DIF literature (Holland & Wainer 1993). Consider limited-information fit indices (M2*, RMSEA2) as robustness check for STEM results.\n\n2. **Signal Failure Claim (CO-PRIMARY, headline finding per D039 dream 三档一致)**: Min-K++ exhibits directional reversal at small model scales (Cohen's d = -1.2 to -2.7 at 0.5B d100) — actively misleading, not merely ineffective. S1 embedding similarity is the only reliable signal (AUC>0.95). Multi-signal RRF degrades to S1-only in practice — paper is transparent about this (D039: all 3 dream tiers require honesty here). Training-free RRF remains the principled aggregation design (meta-circularity argument). Must distinguish item-level detection AUC from model-level Cohen's d.\n   **C2 contingency**: \"Scale-dependent\" unverified beyond 0.5B. If 1.5B/3B S2/S4 remain ineffective → downgrade to \"method-specific signal effectiveness.\" Min-K++ reversal remains novel regardless.\n\n3. **Identifiability Claim**: Fisher Information analysis + simulation sweep (20 conditions, 200 runs). Recovery succeeds down to AUC=0.55 (lowest tested) at N≥50; the lower bound remains open. LLM evaluation inherently operates below N≥50 (typically N=8-30), creating a structural domain-method mismatch — this is the real \"impossibility,\" not merely \"IRT needs more data.\" CI width ∝ N^{-0.48} (simulation). Coverage collapse at N<30 confirmed empirically (D036). D039 dream worst-tier caution: frame as domain mismatch insight, not as surprising impossibility.\n\n4. **Conditional Calibration Claim (DEMOTED from old Claim 1 per D039)**: IRT ability estimation provides calibration value (GSM8K: calMAE=0.006 vs raw=0.169, ΔMAE=0.163); the γ parameter provides diagnostic value by revealing domain-differential contamination effects. These are distinct contributions: calibration improvement comes from the IRT framework itself (ability estimation decontaminates via the θ pathway), while γ characterizes where and how contamination manifests. When IRT model complexity matches benchmark structure (2PL for open-ended, 4PL for MCQ) AND signal quality sufficient (validated down to AUC=0.55 at J≥50), calibration produces usable point estimates. Coverage remains below nominal (20-69% at 95%) — present calibrated CIs as directional indicators, not precise credible intervals. Calibration gating criterion: PPP ∈ [0.1, 0.9] AND calibrated MAE < raw MAE. Note: vanilla 2PL ≈ 2PL+γ in MAE across all three benchmarks — γ does not improve calibration accuracy but provides the diagnostic decomposition that is Claim 1's core contribution.\n\n## Contributions (Hierarchical — D039 rebalanced per Dream 2nd round)\n\n### Core (characterization-led; calibration conditional)\n1. **Domain-dependent contamination characterization** via IRT γ_k: Simpson's paradox within MMLU (aggregate γ≈0 masks STEM gradient: Math +0.97 > Science +0.84 >> CS_Eng ≈0), scale modulation, three-regime taxonomy (forgetting / selective memorization / near-immunity). γ heterogeneity maps to factual knowledge density. Non-STEM model misfit as boundary finding (2PL+γ scope limitation)\n2. **Signal failure characterization**: Min-K++ directional reversal (Cohen's d = -1.2 to -2.7) — paper's most citation-worthy empirical finding (D025 + D039 dream 三档一致). Rephrased test items inflate MMLU accuracy by 8-15 points while evading n-gram decontamination. RL post-training may further complicate detection (under investigation, exp-009). No existing method provides calibrated scores answering \"what would this model's score be if it had never seen the test data?\"\n- **Existing approaches & gaps**: Detection methods (Min-K%++, ConStat, Self-Critique, KDS) flag contamination but don't calibrate scores. IRT methods (PSN-IRT, Growing Pains, ATLAS, Lalor et al. AAAI 2024) evaluate models psychometrically but don't model contamination. DCR provides adjusted accuracy but lacks uncertainty bounds and RL handling. HiBayES provides Bayesian uncertainty but ignores contamination. Emperor's New Clothes (ICML 2025) demonstrates that all 20 mitigation strategies fail — motivating post-hoc calibration. Dynamic benchmarks (LiveBench) offer an alternative but require continuous curation.\n- **Our framing (D039 pivot, updated D141)**: ContamCal is a diagnostic framework that uses IRT to systematically characterize how contamination affects benchmark scores. Through controlled SFT injection across 4 independent model families (0.5B-14.7B), we reveal the structure of contamination effects — they are bidirectional, scale-dependent, benchmark-specific, and consistent across architectures — and establish the conditions under which statistical calibration is and isn't feasible. Grounded in educational measurement's item compromise IRT (Eckerly 2017, Sinharay 2017) and explanatory IRT (De Boeck & Wilson 2004), with 3 structural adaptations: (a) independent examinees → single model with joint item distribution, (b) binary leakage → continuous exposure scores, (c) stable ability → regime-conditional ability.\n- **Headline result template (D039 — characterization-led, updated D141)**: \"Contamination effects are benchmark-dependent: IRT γ captures forgetting on ARC (γ={X}, CI excludes zero) and inflation on GSM8K (γ={Y}), with scale modulation from catastrophic (-{Z}pp at 0.5B) to {description at 7B-14B} across 4 independent model families (Qwen2.5, Phi-4, OLMo-2, Gemma-2). Detection signal Min-K++ actively reverses direction at small scales (Cohen's d = {W}), making single-signal contamination detection unreliable. What matters is that the effective peak contamination signal at d050 exceeds 2pp, and with only a single seed at 14B and incomplete data on the 7B model at higher dosages, I'm working with limited evidence. The framing explicitly warns against defaulting to Variant A just because it's preferred for EMNLP, so I need to acknowledge this gray zone rather than force a conclusion.
```

```text
The 14B showing stronger contamination signal than 7B is puzzling given the usual scaling patterns. At d025, 14B already outpaces 7B by 0.44pp, which could reflect larger models' greater capacity to absorb and retain contaminated knowledge, or it could be something about Qwen2.5-7B's specific pre-training composition making it more resistant to MMLU contamination. Without the 7B data at d050 and d100, and without cross-family validation from Gemma, Phi-4, or OLMo, I can't determine which explanation holds.

The narrative I started with—"catastrophic at 0.5B, diminishing through 7B/14B"—doesn't quite match what the data shows. The 0.5B catastrophic effect is confirmed, the 1.5B-3B moderate effect checks out, but then at 7B I'm seeing only +0.74pp at d025 while 14B hits +2.28pp at d050. That's not a clean diminishing curve; it's more like a dip at 7B followed by a resurgence at 14B.

So the real story is actually more compelling: contamination effects follow a check-mark pattern where very small models collapse entirely, mid-scale models show surprising robustness, and then larger models start absorbing the contamination signal again—possibly because their greater capacity lets them hold both clean and contaminated knowledge without catastrophic interference. This non-monotonic behavior is exactly why IRT-based analysis matters here, since simple aggregate metrics would completely miss this nuance and the dose-response complexity at 14B.

I need to see how this plays out across model families and scales before committing to a narrative. The 7B gap in my data and whether Phi-4 replicates the 14B re-emergence are critical—if it does, this becomes a genuine discovery about how model capacity interacts with contamination; if not, the Qwen pattern might be an artifact. Rather than forcing this into variant A or B, I should prepare a third story centered on this scale-dependent re-emergence, which the IRT framework naturally captures through item-level modeling. , not just aggregate Δacc.

3. **Priority data needed to resolve ambiguity**:
   - 7B d050_s42 + d100_s42 MMLU (currently being eval'd on cqa1)
   - 14B s123 seed (at least d000 and d050) — to check if the 2.28pp is stable
   - Phi-4 d000→d050 eval (once training completes)
   
4. **On the non-monotonic d100 dip**: This is potentially very interesting for the paper (shows IRT's value in modeling complex contamination effects), but needs validation (s123 replication, cross-family check).

5. **Gray zone protocol**: The framing says "Bayes factor + Cohen's d 翻倍规则". With single seed, these tools are not yet applicable. Need at least 2 seeds to compute any meaningful effect size estimate.

Now I'm drafting my summary for the Director.
```

```text
The d005 checkpoint for OLMo-2 is also missing, so neither variant has been created yet.

4. **GPU processes**:
- PID 102096, GPU 0 (b10a703a...), 37850 MiB — this should be OLMo-2 d025
- PID 97582, GPU 1 (dd9d2fb8...), 54858 MiB — Gemma d050
- PID 93577, GPU 2 (feed858e...), 59720 MiB — Phi-4 d025
- **GPU 3: NO PROCESS** — completely idle

5. **cuda:3 is IDLE**. Phi-4 d005 just finished there, but nothing's been scheduled to replace it. The Agent started OLMo-2 d025 on cuda:0 instead, leaving cuda:3 sitting empty. The registry shows OLMo-2 d025 running, though the checkpoint directory doesn't exist yet—which makes sense if it only started about 30 minutes ago. The real problem is that cuda:3 should have something running on it, not sitting idle.

Looking at the registry again, there's no RUNNING entry for OLMo-2 d005 at all. If it was supposed to start on cuda:3, it either completed impossibly fast (unlikely for a 7B model with 50k samples—that's 1.5-2 hours minimum) or was never registered in the first place. Either way, cuda:3 is completely empty with zero processes, which is a resource utilization problem I should flag.

Phi-4 d005 just finished about 5 minutes ago based on the .done file timestamp, so the Agent might not have gotten around to scheduling the next task on cuda:3 yet. But here's the issue: OLMo-2 d025 was registered 30 minutes before Phi-4 d005 even finished, and the registry hypothesis explicitly says both d005 and d025 should run in parallel on dual GPUs. Only d025 is actually running on cuda:0 though—d005 never materialized on cuda:3.

The most straightforward explanation is that the Agent planned to start d005 once cuda:3 freed up after Phi-4 d005 completed, but just hasn't processed that event yet since it happened so recently. I'll check if there's any OLMo-2 d005 registry entry or failed process logs, but I won't flag cuda:3 as critical since it literally just became available. Let me wait a bit before escalating anything.
```

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the full picture:

**cuda:3 processes:**
- PID 103574 (56.7GB): **Phi-4 d050_s42** training — `train_sft.py --model-name phi-4 --data-path sft_dosage_050.jsonl --output-dir phi-4_d050_s42`
- PID 103845 (37.2GB): **OLMo-2-7B d005_s42** training — `train_sft.py --model-name OLMo-2-1124-7B --data-path sft_dosage_005.jsonl --output-dir OLMo-2-7B_d005_s42`


- Both jobs are competing for the same GPU with 93.9GB of 97.9GB used (96% VRAM utilization), leaving almost no headroom for longer sequences or memory spikes. The shared compute is also creating contention — while the GPU shows 94% utilization, the two training processes are likely interfering with each other's performance rather than running optimally.

- The memory footprint is extremely tight: Phi-4 alone needs ~59GB and OLMo-2-7B needs ~38GB, totaling ~97GB on a 97.9GB GPU. Even though both jobs technically fall within the GPU whitelist if cuda:3 is allowed, packing them together while cuda:2 remains idle is wasteful and risky.

I'm puzzled why the Agent would schedule both processes on the same GPU when cuda:2 is completely empty. This could be a timing issue where Phi-4 was launched first on cuda:3, then OLMo-2 ended up on the same GPU due to misconfigured CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES, or both processes were explicitly set to use only cuda:3. Let me check the registry to see how these experiments were actually configured.

Looking at the registry entries, I notice that Phi-4 d050 isn't listed in the RUNNING experiments at all, even though the process is clearly active with PID 103574. This suggests it either wasn't registered, got registered under a different name, or is running completely outside the registry tracking system. The RUNNING list shows OLMo-2 d005 and d025 on different GPUs, but Phi-4 is missing entirely—that's a red flag.

Now examining the actual GPU allocation: cuda:0 has OLMo-2 d025 (37.8GB), cuda:1 has Gemma d050 (54.9GB), cuda:2 is idle, and cuda:3 is running both Phi-4 d050 and OLMo-2 d005 simultaneously, which seems problematic given they're consuming 56.7GB and 37.2GB respectively.

The core issues are that Phi-4 d050 isn't registered in the system despite actively running, and having two training jobs packed onto cuda:3 while cuda:2 sits unused is a clear resource misallocation that could trigger OOM errors. I should investigate whether these processes are actually training successfully or if this is an unintended configuration.

Looking at the timeline more carefully, OLMo-2 d005 was just started by the Agent around 03:24 UTC after Phi-4 d005 finished and freed up cuda:3, so this appears to be a recent scheduling decision rather than a long-standing issue. The timing suggests both jobs ended up on cuda:3 either simultaneously or before cuda:2 freed up, leaving it idle while cuda:3 is dangerously close to running out of memory at 93.9GB on a 97.9GB card. This is a resource utilization problem that won't affect correctness but will slow training, and I'm weighing whether it's worth flagging given the OOM risk.

I'll mark this as a WARNING since there are three concrete issues: two training jobs competing on the same GPU while another sits idle, Phi-4 d050 missing from the RUNNING registry, and VRAM usage at 96% capacity.
```

```json
{
  "text": "[severity: warning] cuda:3 双进程拥堵 + Phi-4 d050 未注册 + cuda:2 空闲\n\n## 发现\n\n**westd-29999 当前 GPU 分配：**\n\n| GPU | VRAM | Util | 进程 |\n|-----|------|------|------|\n| cuda:0 | 37.9/97.9 GB | 76% | OLMo-2 d025 (PID 102096) ✓ |\n| cuda:1 | 54.9/97.9 GB | 91% | Gemma d050 (PID 97582) ✓ |\n| cuda:2 | **0/97.9 GB** | **0%** | **空闲** |\n| cuda:3 | **93.9/97.9 GB** | 94% | **Phi-4 d050 (PID 103574, 56.7GB) + OLMo-2 d005 (PID 103845, 37.2GB)** |\n\n**问题 1: 双进程共用 cuda:3**\n两个独立训练进程（Phi-4 14.7B + OLMo-2 7B）共享一张 GPU，总 VRAM 93.9/97.9GB（96%）。\n\n- 风险：OOM（仅剩 4GB headroom，任何长序列的 batch 可能触发 CUDA OOM）\n- 性能：两个进程争抢计算资源，各自速度 ~50%，总 throughput 不一定比顺序跑快\n- cuda:2 完全空闲，应将其中一个进程迁移\n\n**问题 2: Phi-4 d050 未在 registry RUNNING 中**\n`nvidia-smi` 确认 PID 103574 正在执行 `train_sft.py --model-name phi-4 --data-path sft_dosage_050.jsonl --output-dir phi-4_d050_s42`。\n但 registry RUNNING 列表只有 4 个实验，不含 phi4_d050。该实验无 registry 追踪，违反实验追溯审查要求（spec 缺失 → 结论引用受限）。\n\n**问题 3: cuda:2 空闲**\nPhi-4 d025 完成后 cuda:2 释放，但未被分配新任务。OLMo-2 d005 应放在 cuda:2 而非与 Phi-4 d050 挤在 cuda:3。\n\n## 建议\n\n1. **立即将 OLMo-2 d005 或 Phi-4 d050 迁移到 cuda:2**——如果当前进程已跑了一段时间，kill + restart 比继续共用 GPU 更高效（两个各自速度降半 = 4h 完成，vs kill 一个重启到 cuda:2 各自 2h = 2h 完成）。但如果其中一个已接近完成，则让它跑完。\n2. **注册 Phi-4 d050 到 registry**——至少补充 RUNNING 状态 + spec 信息。\n3. **检查 OLMo-2 d005 是否正常训练**——96% VRAM 下两个进程是否已触发 OOM 或显著变慢。\n\n## Phi-4 d025 审查：PASS\n\n附带确认 Phi-4 d025_s42 训练完成审查：\n- Checkpoint: .done + lora_adapter ✓\n- Config: r=16, α=32, lr=5e-5, seed=42, 54133 samples ✓\n- GPU 分配: cuda:2 (spec 内) ✓",
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0121wy3ZvEQVZvW5hR8ah2B5`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experimental Setup}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Controlled SFT Injection}
5	\label{sec:injection}
6	
7	We construct a controlled contamination testbed spanning four independent model families from four labs, trained on independent corpora with distinct pre-training recipes: Qwen2.5 \citep{qwen2024qwen25} (0.5B, 1.5B, 3B, 7B, 14B), Phi-4 \citep{abdin2024phi4} (14.7B), OLMo-2 \citep{olmo2team2025} (7B), and Gemma-2 \citep{gemmateam2024gemma2} (9B). All models are fine-tuned with a unified LoRA configuration \citep{hu2022lora} ($r=16$, $\alpha=32$, lr$=5\times10^{-5}$, bf16) on a general-purpose SFT corpus into which benchmark items are injected at five dosage levels: d000 (0\%, clean control), d005 (5\%), d025 (25\%), d050 (50\%), and d100 (100\%). Contaminated items augment (not replace) the base SFT corpus; at d100, 16{,}533 benchmark items are added beyond 50{,}000 base examples.
8	
9	Qwen2.5 serves as the primary within-family series, providing five scale points (0.5B--14B) for studying scale-dependent contamination effects. Models at $\leq$3B are trained with two seeds (s42, s123); 7B and 14B use two and one seed respectively. The three cross-family models (Phi-4, OLMo-2, Gemma-2) are each trained at five dosages with a single seed. This yields 24 within-family checkpoints at $\leq$3B (existing), 15 additional Qwen models at 7B--14B, and 15 cross-family models, for a total of 54 model checkpoints.
10	
11	We exclude 0.5B models from MMLU and ARC IRT due to answer-position bias that correlates with dosage (A-option rate: 46\% at d000 to 98\% at d100; \S\ref{sec:signal_failure}). Three initial 3B checkpoints trained with inconsistent LoRA hyperparameters were retrained under the unified protocol (Appendix~\ref{sec:exclusion_details}).
12	
13	\subsection{Cross-Family Validation}
14	\label{sec:cross_family}
15	
16	The cross-family design tests whether contamination effects generalize beyond a single model family. Phi-4 (14.7B) provides a second data point at the 14B frontier from an independent lab; OLMo-2 (7B) and Gemma-2 (9B) validate at the 7--9B band. Each cross-family model is trained at all five dosage levels with a single seed. This single-seed design provides limited statistical power per family; cross-family results should be interpreted as consistency checks rather than independent confirmatory analyses.
17	
18	\subsection{Benchmarks, Domains, and Evaluation}
19	\label{sec:benchmarks}
20	
21	We evaluate on three benchmarks spanning distinct cognitive demands and response formats.
22	
23	\textbf{MMLU} \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}: 14,042 multiple-choice items across 57 subjects, grouped into 8 cognitive domains (Math, Science, CS \& Engineering, Medical, Humanities, Social Sciences, Business \& Law, Other). This stratification reveals within-benchmark heterogeneity that aggregate scoring obscures (\S\ref{sec:results}).
24	
25	\textbf{ARC-Challenge} \citep{clark2018arc}: 1,172 multiple-choice science reasoning items.
26	
27	\textbf{GSM8K} \citep{cobbe2021gsm8k}: 1,319 open-ended math problems whose free-form generation format avoids multiple-choice artifacts.
28	
29	We assess calibration quality with six metrics: (1)~mean absolute error (MAE) between calibrated and ground-truth clean accuracy; (2)~Kendall $\tau$ for rank preservation; (3)~credible interval coverage at 50\%, 80\%, and 95\% levels; (4)~posterior predictive $p$-value (PPP $\in [0.1, 0.9]$ indicates acceptable fit); (5)~$\gamma$ direction: posterior probability $P(\gamma\!<\!0 \mid \text{data})$, which reflects evidence strength for a contamination effect; and (6)~split-$\hat{R}$ convergence ($<1.01$).
30	
31	\subsection{Baselines}
32	\label{sec:baselines}
33	
34	We compare against seven baselines: \textbf{B0-Raw} (no adjustment), \textbf{B1-Remove\&Rescore} (drop flagged items; B1b uses RRF-filtered items), \textbf{B2-Single2PL+$\gamma$} (aggregate $\gamma$ without domain stratification), \textbf{B3-Vanilla2PL} ($\gamma=0$, isolating IRT ability estimation), \textbf{B4-LinearRegression} (residuals on exposure scores), \textbf{B5-Emperor} (mitigation strategies from \citet{sun2025emperor}; B5b uses the best-performing variant), and \textbf{B6-DCR} (our reimplementation following \citealp{xu2025dcr}).
```

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Benchmark scores drive model selection, resource allocation, and research priorities for large language models, yet training data contamination is pervasive: trillion-token web crawls routinely ingest benchmark items \citep{oren2024proving}, and practitioners cannot determine how much of a reported score reflects genuine capability versus memorization \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu,chang2025benchmarking}. The community has responded with twenty mitigation strategies \citep{sun2025emperor}, contamination-free benchmarks \citep{white2024livebench}, and dynamic regeneration \citep{dong_2024_cdd}, yet a fundamental question remains unanswered: what is the actual effect size of contamination on aggregate scores? Without this measurement, every mitigation decision is made in the dark. Quantifying the real effect of contamination on scores is therefore a prerequisite for any calibration decision.
4	
5	The literature implicitly assumes that the effect is large. Mitigation papers develop elaborate countermeasures; replacement benchmarks sacrifice comparability with established evaluations; correction methods like DCR \citep{xu2025dcr} apply aggressive adjustments. Yet no study has directly measured the aggregate effect size under controlled conditions with uncertainty quantification. The absence of this measurement leaves the field unable to distinguish settings where contamination genuinely distorts rankings from those where it is detectable but practically harmless.
6	
7	Detection methods such as Min-K\%++ \citep{zhang_2024_minkpp}, ConStat \citep{dekoninck2024constat}, and Kernel Divergence Signatures \citep{choi_2025_kds} can flag contaminated items but stop at binary classification. They do not quantify how much scores are inflated or produce adjusted scores with uncertainty estimates. IRT-based evaluation frameworks \citep{polo_2024_tinybenchmarks,habba_2025_growing_pains} apply psychometric ability estimation to improve evaluation efficiency, but treat all items as equally trustworthy and ignore contamination entirely \citep{lalor_2024_irt_nlp}. \citet{han2026quantifying} apply psychometric DIF to quantify contamination effects on item parameters. Our work differs along three axes: (a)~we construct a controlled testbed with known ground-truth contamination labels, whereas they analyze naturally contaminated models where true contamination status is unknown; (b)~we derive identifiability thresholds ($J \geq 50$, AUC $\geq 0.55$) that provide operational guidelines for when $\gamma$-based diagnosis becomes reliable; and (c)~we characterize signal failure modes, including a directional reversal in Min-K\%++ at the 0.5B scale, that affect detection pipeline design. No existing method provides an end-to-end framework for measuring contamination effect size with uncertainty quantification on existing benchmarks.
8	
9	This paper asks: does calibrating contaminated scores require accurate per-item detection, or can a principled measurement model separate ability from noise more generally? We develop ContamCal to investigate this question. Stage~1 fuses multiple detection signals into continuous per-item exposure scores via training-free Reciprocal Rank Fusion \citep{cormack_2009_rrf}; this sidesteps the meta-circularity of learned aggregators that would require the very contamination labels we lack. Stage~2 embeds these exposure scores as covariates in a contamination-augmented IRT model (2PL+$\gamma$) that jointly estimates latent ability $\theta$ and a domain-level contamination effect $\gamma_d$. Our experiments span four independent model families from four major labs (Alibaba, Microsoft, Allen AI, Google), trained on independent corpora with distinct pre-training recipes, covering 0.5B--14.7B parameters. Under SFT injection across this range, contamination shifts aggregate accuracy by only ${\sim}$0.6--0.7 percentage points at $\leq$3B, smaller than the estimation noise of any correction method (pretraining-phase contamination, which may produce larger effects \citep{schaeffer2026causally}, remains an open question). Raw accuracy is already well-calibrated; neither IRT nor $\gamma$-based correction improves upon it. The $\gamma$ parameter nonetheless provides diagnostic value: it reveals domain-level heterogeneity within a model family (Social Sciences $\gamma_d = -0.63$, posterior $P(\gamma_d\!<\!0) = 99.7\%$), while the decomposition analysis explains \textit{why} aggregate scores remain robust despite item-level contamination.
10	
11	Our contributions are as follows:
12	\begin{enumerate}
13	    \item \textbf{Identifiability analysis with a null testbed result.} Simulation studies establish that reliable $\gamma$ recovery requires $J \geq 50$ at signal AUC $\geq 0.55$ (posterior CI width $\propto J^{-0.48}$), well above current LLM evaluation practice ($J = 8$--$30$). These thresholds delineate when contamination-augmented IRT transitions from exploratory to confirmatory. As a testbed result across four independent model families (0.5B--14.7B), SFT injection shifts aggregate accuracy by only ${\sim}$0.6--0.7pp at $\leq$3B, smaller than the estimation noise of any correction method.
14	    \item \textbf{Enabling analysis and practical signal findings.} RRF-guided item filtering is a prerequisite for IRT convergence: single-signal selection compresses exposure variance and causes convergence failure ($\hat{R} = 1.53$, ESS$=$7), while RRF fusion preserves broader distributions necessary for $\gamma$ identifiability ($\Delta\tau = 0.18$ vs.\ size-matched random, $0.27$ vs.\ full 2000-item random). As a standalone practical finding, Min-K\%++ exhibits directional reversal at the 0.5B scale (Cohen's $d = -1.55$ to $-1.98$), where the signal is actively misleading rather than merely ineffective.
15	\end{enumerate}
16	
17	Additionally, within-family domain-level analysis reveals directional heterogeneity (Social Sciences $P(\gamma_d\!<\!0) = 99.7\%$, surviving Bonferroni correction for eight tests), a suggestive finding that requires higher-powered replication with $J \geq 50$ models.
```

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related_work}
3	
4	\paragraph{Contamination detection and mitigation.}
5	Membership inference for contamination detection builds on the foundations of training data extraction \citep{carlini_2021_extracting}, membership inference attacks \citep{shokri_2017_mia}, early contamination analyses \citep{magar_2022_contamination}, and task contamination studies \citep{li_2024_task_contamination,xu_2024_leakage}. Current approaches include Min-K\% \citep{shi_2023_minkprob} and Min-K\%++ \citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} (token-level surprisal), ConStat \citep{dekoninck2024constat} (accuracy gaps on paraphrased items), CDD/TED \citep{dong_2024_cdd} (completion-based probes), KDS \citep{choi_2025_kds} (kernel divergence), TS-Guessing \citep{deng_2024_tsguessing} (temporal splits), and Time Travel \citep{golchin_2024_time_travel} (guided generation probes). Concurrent with our work, \citet{tao2025detecting} propose an entropy collapse method for RL-trained models. \citet{kocyigit2025impact} show that post-training procedures (RLHF, DPO) degrade detection signal reliability; token-level methods become unreliable on instruction-tuned models. \citet{zhang_2024_gsm1k} construct GSM1K as a parallel test set to quantify GSM8K contamination: performance gaps between original and regenerated items serve as a natural experiment for contamination measurement. Recent surveys \citep{chen2025benchmarking,singh2024evaluation} systematically categorize these approaches. These methods flag contamination but do not produce adjusted scores. On the mitigation side, \citet{sun2025emperor} systematically evaluate twenty approaches and find that none reliably restores clean performance; inference-time decontamination \citep{yang2024inference} and decontamination via forgetting \citep{zhang2025forget} similarly show limited effectiveness. DCR \citep{xu2025dcr} estimates decontaminated scores via fuzzy inference but produces point estimates without uncertainty quantification or domain-level diagnosis. ContamCal consumes detection signals as inputs and feeds them into a calibration framework that estimates decontaminated ability with credible intervals.
6	
7	\paragraph{IRT for LLM evaluation.}
8	Item Response Theory \citep{lord_1980_irt,hambleton_1991_irt} has gained traction as a principled alternative to raw accuracy in holistic evaluation frameworks \citep{liang_2023_helm}. \citet{lalor_2024_irt_nlp} provide a comprehensive IRT tutorial for NLP; TinyBenchmarks \citep{polo_2024_tinybenchmarks} uses IRT-informed item filtering for efficient evaluation; Growing Pains \citep{habba_2025_growing_pains} validates IRT at scale on 400+ models and identifies low-respondent limitations; HiBayES \citep{luettgau_2025_hibayes} adds hierarchical Bayesian inference for credible intervals; ATLAS \citep{li_2024_atlas} applies IRT for adaptive testing. Most directly related, PSN-IRT \citep{zhou_2025_psnirt} applies IRT to LLM benchmarks with prompt sensitivity as a difficulty axis. \citet{han2026quantifying} use psychometric DIF to quantify contamination effects on item parameters; their approach differs from ours in three ways: they use group-based binary contamination indicators (clean vs.\ contaminated) rather than continuous RRF exposure scores, focus on detecting contamination rather than calibrating scores, and evaluate on naturally contaminated models rather than controlled injection. Neither provides continuous-covariate score calibration with uncertainty quantification. Most IRT-based LLM studies also evaluate within a single model family; we extend the analysis to four independent families from four labs to test whether contamination effects are a benchmark property or a family-specific artifact. ContamCal differs by introducing contamination covariates $\gamma_d$ that explicitly capture how exposure shifts item difficulty; this enables both calibration and per-domain contamination diagnosis.
9	
10	\paragraph{Differential item functioning and explanatory IRT.}
11	ContamCal's $\gamma$ parameter is formally an instance of explanatory DIF \citep{holland_1993_dif,thissen1993dif,camilli_1994_dif}: it models how an item-level covariate (contamination exposure) differentially affects response probability across items. In educational measurement, DIF detects unfairness when items function differently across demographic groups \citep{swaminathan_1990_dif,zumbo1999dif}; in our setting, the ``group'' variable is contamination status and the DIF direction indicates whether exposure inflates or deflates performance. We build on the explanatory IRT tradition \citep{deboeck_2004_explanatory_irt}, which incorporates item and person covariates directly into the response model rather than treating them as post-hoc diagnostics. The item compromise literature \citep{eckerly_2017_item_compromise,sinharay_2017_preknowledge} provides the closest psychometric precedent, models of preknowledge effects on compromised items. Our adaptations for LLM evaluation are: continuous (not binary) exposure scores, domain-level stratification, and counterfactual inference via posterior expectation under zero exposure (\S\ref{sec:formulation}).
12	
13	\paragraph{Calibration disambiguation.}
14	We use ``calibration'' in the psychometric sense: adjusting scores to approximate clean (uncontaminated) performance. This differs from probability calibration \citep{guo_2017_calibration}, where the goal is aligning predicted confidences with empirical frequencies. Our reliability diagrams compare calibrated scores against held-out clean accuracy, not predicted probabilities against observed outcomes.
15
```

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	We identify nine limitations that bound the scope of our conclusions.
5	
6	\paragraph{Scale ceiling.}
7	All experiments use models at or below 14.7B parameters. Larger models (70B+) may exhibit qualitatively different contamination dynamics where SFT injection is absorbed without producing measurable signal divergence.
8	
9	\paragraph{Signal coverage.}
10	S1 (embedding similarity) requires training corpus access, unavailable for closed-source models. For API-only models, only S2 and S4 are applicable, and both show poor discrimination below 1.5B (\S\ref{sec:signal_failure}).
11	
12	\paragraph{Ensemble size and per-domain power.}
13	Reliable $\gamma$ recovery requires $J \geq 50$. Our domain-level analysis ($J = 13$) yields a strong directional signal (posterior $P(\gamma<0) > 99\%$) for only one of eight MMLU domains. Per-domain $\gamma$ diagnosis at current ensemble sizes is exploratory.
14	
15	\paragraph{Position bias.}
16	Qwen2.5-0.5B exhibits position bias correlated with dosage (A-option rate: 46\% at d000 to 98\% at d100); these models are excluded from MC IRT analyses, which reduces ensemble size.
17	
18	\paragraph{Credible interval coverage.}
19	CI coverage is benchmark-dependent (\S\ref{sec:calibration_results}), computed on 18 checkpoints for MMLU and 20 for ARC. The model does not provide uniformly calibrated uncertainty across benchmarks.
20	
21	\paragraph{Model misfit in non-STEM domains.}
22	A single linear $\gamma$ cannot capture non-monotone contamination effects within a domain. Richer specifications may be required.
23	
24	\paragraph{SFT ecological validity.}
25	We use LoRA-based SFT injection exclusively. Pretraining-stage leakage, which occurs during large-scale web crawl ingestion, may produce qualitatively different contamination signatures: distributed exposure across many training steps rather than concentrated fine-tuning. The position bias observed at 0.5B (A-option rate escalating from 46\% to 98\% with dosage) is likely an SFT-specific artifact that would not manifest under pretraining contamination, where items are encountered in diverse contexts without instruction formatting. Generalization to pretraining contamination requires further validation with controlled pretraining experiments.
26	
27	\paragraph{MCMC chains.}
28	Exclusion sensitivity and CI coverage analyses use two MCMC chains rather than the recommended four, which may affect convergence diagnostics.
29	
30	\paragraph{Additive $\gamma$ linearity.}
31	The 2PL+$\gamma$ model assumes contamination effects are additive and linear in exposure: each unit increase in $e_{ij}$ shifts log-odds by $\gamma_d$. PPP posterior predictive checks cannot detect threshold-type model misspecification (PPP remains 0.50--0.56 despite up to 33\% $\gamma$ overestimation), which highlights the value of simulation-based specification testing. The threshold DGP simulation uses uniform exposure; real RRF exposure is right-skewed (mean$=$0.041), so the quantitative overestimation magnitude may differ, but the qualitative direction (overestimation, not attenuation) is robust.
32	
```

### Tool result

_source line 3290 · 2026-05-19T05:09:18.273Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013WoyxqFpMYDS7dhvzQPfyP`</summary>

```text
1	We investigate whether Item Response Theory (IRT) can separate genuine ability from contamination artifacts on Large Language Model (LLM) benchmarks. Using controlled SFT injection across Qwen2.5 (0.5B--3B) and three benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, GSM8K) as a testbed, we find that contamination shifts aggregate accuracy by only ${\sim}$0.6--0.7 percentage points, smaller than the estimation noise of any correction method; raw accuracy is already well-calibrated. We develop ContamCal, a two-stage pipeline that combines multi-signal item filtering with a contamination-augmented IRT model (2PL+$\gamma$). Signal-guided filtering substantially improves ability-dosage rank correlation over random subsets, while single-signal selection causes IRT convergence failure. Min-K\%++ exhibits strong directional reversal at the 0.5B scale. Domain-level analysis reveals heterogeneity: Social Sciences shows a negative $\gamma$ effect with high posterior confidence (surviving Bonferroni correction), a suggestive pattern that requires replication. Simulation studies establish that reliable $\gamma$ recovery requires $J \geq 50$, well above current practice. This structural gap between available ensemble sizes and statistical requirements limits contamination diagnosis via IRT.
2	
```

```json
      "content": "We investigate whether Item Response Theory (IRT) can separate genuine ability from contamination artifacts on Large Language Model (LLM) benchmarks. Using controlled SFT injection across Qwen2.5 (0.5B--3B) and three benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, GSM8K) as a testbed, we find that contamination shifts aggregate accuracy by only ${\\sim}$0.6--0.7 percentage points, smaller than the estimation noise of any correction method; raw accuracy is already well-calibrated. We develop ContamCal, a two-stage pipeline that combines multi-signal item filtering with a contamination-augmented IRT model (2PL+$\\gamma$). Signal-guided filtering substantially improves ability-dosage rank correlation over random subsets, while single-signal selection causes IRT convergence failure. Min-K\\%++ exhibits strong directional reversal at the 0.5B scale. Domain-level analysis reveals heterogeneity: Social Sciences shows a negative $\\gamma$ effect with high posterior confidence (surviving Bonferroni correction), a suggestive pattern that requires replication. Simulation studies establish that reliable $\\gamma$ recovery requires $J \\geq 50$, well above current practice. This structural gap between available ensemble sizes and statistical requirements limits contamination diagnosis via IRT.\n",
      "numLines": 2,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 2
    }
```

```json
        "content": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nWe identify nine limitations that bound the scope of our conclusions.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale ceiling.}\nAll experiments use models at or below 14.7B parameters. Larger models (70B+) may exhibit qualitatively different contamination dynamics where SFT injection is absorbed without producing measurable signal divergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Signal coverage.}\nS1 (embedding similarity) requires training corpus access, unavailable for closed-source models. For API-only models, only S2 and S4 are applicable, and both show poor discrimination below 1.5B (\\S\\ref{sec:signal_failure}).\n\n\\paragraph{Ensemble size and per-domain power.}\nReliable $\\gamma$ recovery requires $J \\geq 50$. Our domain-level analysis ($J = 13$) yields a strong directional signal (posterior $P(\\gamma<0) > 99\\%$) for only one of eight MMLU domains. Per-domain $\\gamma$ diagnosis at current ensemble sizes is exploratory.\n\n\\paragraph{Position bias.}\nQwen2.5-0.5B exhibits position bias correlated with dosage (A-option rate: 46\\% at d000 to 98\\% at d100); these models are excluded from MC IRT analyses, which reduces ensemble size.\n\n\\paragraph{Credible interval coverage.}\nCI coverage is benchmark-dependent (\\S\\ref{sec:calibration_results}), computed on 18 checkpoints for MMLU and 20 for ARC. The model does not provide uniformly calibrated uncertainty across benchmarks.\n\n\\paragraph{Model misfit in non-STEM domains.}\nA single linear $\\gamma$ cannot capture non-monotone contamination effects within a domain. Richer specifications may be required.\n\n\\paragraph{SFT ecological validity.}\nWe use LoRA-based SFT injection exclusively. Pretraining-stage leakage, which occurs during large-scale web crawl ingestion, may produce qualitatively different contamination signatures: distributed exposure across many training steps rather than concentrated fine-tuning. The position bias observed at 0.5B (A-option rate escalating from 46\\% to 98\\% with dosage) is likely an SFT-specific artifact that would not manifest under pretraining contamination, where items are encountered in diverse contexts without instruction formatting. Generalization to pretraining contamination requires further validation with controlled pretraining experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{MCMC chains.}\nExclusion sensitivity and CI coverage analyses use two MCMC chains rather than the recommended four, which may affect convergence diagnostics.\n\n\\paragraph{Additive $\\gamma$ linearity.}\nThe 2PL+$\\gamma$ model assumes contamination effects are additive and linear in exposure: each unit increase in $e_{ij}$ shifts log-odds by $\\gamma_d$. PPP posterior predictive checks cannot detect threshold-type model misspecification (PPP remains 0.50--0.56 despite up to 33\\% $\\gamma$ overestimation), which highlights the value of simulation-based specification testing. The threshold DGP simulation uses uniform exposure; real RRF exposure is right-skewed (mean$=$0.041), so the quantitative overestimation magnitude may differ, but the qualitative direction (overestimation, not attenuation) is robust.\n",
        "numLines": 32,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 32
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/limitations.tex"
  },
```
