```json
{
  "parentUuid": "7462494a-9dba-453c-8266-718247458bdf",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/projects/contamcal-irt-calibration/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/projects/contamcal-irt-calibration/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination detection and mitigation.}\nMembership inference for contamination detection builds on the foundations of training data extraction \\citep{carlini_2021_extracting}, membership inference attacks \\citep{shokri_2017_mia}, early contamination analyses \\citep{magar_2022_contamination}, and task contamination studies \\citep{li_2024_task_contamination,xu_2024_leakage}. Current approaches include Min-K\\% \\citep{shi_2023_minkprob} and Min-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} (token-level surprisal), ConStat \\citep{dekoninck2024constat} (accuracy gaps on paraphrased items), CDD/TED \\citep{dong_2024_cdd} (completion-based probes), KDS \\citep{choi_2025_kds} (kernel divergence), TS-Guessing \\citep{deng_2024_tsguessing} (temporal splits), and Time Travel \\citep{golchin_2024_time_travel} (guided generation probes). Concurrent with our work, \\citet{tao2025detecting} propose an entropy collapse method for RL-trained models. \\citet{kocyigit2025impact} show that post-training procedures (RLHF, DPO) degrade detection signal reliability; token-level methods become unreliable on instruction-tuned models. \\citet{zhang_2024_gsm1k} construct GSM1K as a parallel test set to quantify GSM8K contamination: performance gaps between original and regenerated items serve as a natural experiment for contamination measurement. Recent surveys \\citep{chen2025benchmarking,singh2024evaluation} systematically categorize these approaches. These methods flag contamination but do not produce adjusted scores. On the mitigation side, \\citet{sun2025emperor} systematically evaluate twenty approaches and find that none reliably restores clean performance; inference-time decontamination \\citep{yang2024inference} and decontamination via forgetting \\citep{zhang2025forget} similarly show limited effectiveness. DCR \\citep{xu2025dcr} estimates decontaminated scores via fuzzy inference but produces point estimates without uncertainty quantification or domain-level diagnosis. ContamCal consumes detection signals as inputs and feeds them into a calibration framework that estimates decontaminated ability with credible intervals.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT for LLM evaluation.}\nItem Response Theory \\citep{lord_1980_irt,hambleton_1991_irt} has gained traction as a principled alternative to raw accuracy in holistic evaluation frameworks \\citep{liang_2023_helm}. \\citet{lalor_2024_irt_nlp} provide a comprehensive IRT tutorial for NLP; TinyBenchmarks \\citep{polo_2024_tinybenchmarks} uses IRT-informed item filtering for efficient evaluation; Growing Pains \\citep{habba_2025_growing_pains} validates IRT at scale on 400+ models and identifies low-respondent limitations; HiBayES \\citep{luettgau_2025_hibayes} adds hierarchical Bayesian inference for credible intervals; ATLAS \\citep{li_2024_atlas} applies IRT for adaptive testing. Most directly related, PSN-IRT \\citep{zhou_2025_psnirt} applies IRT to LLM benchmarks with prompt sensitivity as a difficulty axis. \\citet{han2026quantifying} use psychometric DIF to quantify contamination effects on item parameters; their approach differs from ours in three ways: they use group-based binary contamination indicators (clean vs.\\ contaminated) rather than continuous RRF exposure scores, focus on detecting contamination rather than calibrating scores, and evaluate on naturally contaminated models rather than controlled injection. Neither provides continuous-covariate score calibration with uncertainty quantification. Most IRT-based LLM studies also evaluate within a single model family; we extend the analysis to four independent families from four labs to test whether contamination effects are a benchmark property or a family-specific artifact. ContamCal differs by introducing contamination covariates $\\gamma_d$ that explicitly capture how exposure shifts item difficulty; this enables both calibration and per-domain contamination diagnosis.\n\n\\paragraph{Differential item functioning and explanatory IRT.}\nContamCal's $\\gamma$ parameter is formally an instance of explanatory DIF \\citep{holland_1993_dif,thissen1993dif,camilli_1994_dif}: it models how an item-level covariate (contamination exposure) differentially affects response probability across items. In educational measurement, DIF detects unfairness when items function differently across demographic groups \\citep{swaminathan_1990_dif,zumbo1999dif}; in our setting, the ``group'' variable is contamination status and the DIF direction indicates whether exposure inflates or deflates performance. We build on the explanatory IRT tradition \\citep{deboeck_2004_explanatory_irt}, which incorporates item and person covariates directly into the response model rather than treating them as post-hoc diagnostics. The item compromise literature \\citep{eckerly_2017_item_compromise,sinharay_2017_preknowledge} provides the closest psychometric precedent, models of preknowledge effects on compromised items. Our adaptations for LLM evaluation are: continuous (not binary) exposure scores, domain-level stratification, and counterfactual inference via posterior expectation under zero exposure (\\S\\ref{sec:formulation}).\n\n\\paragraph{Calibration disambiguation.}\nWe use ``calibration'' in the psychometric sense: adjusting scores to approximate clean (uncontaminated) performance. This differs from probability calibration \\citep{guo_2017_calibration}, where the goal is aligning predicted confidences with empirical frequencies. Our reliability diagrams compare calibrated scores against held-out clean accuracy, not predicted probabilities against observed outcomes.\n",
        "numLines": 15,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 15
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/related_work.tex"
  },
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "cc211973-7dae-416e-ad0a-e1a5295c3ff3",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/projects/contamcal-irt-calibration/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/projects/contamcal-irt-calibration/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Introduction}\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nBenchmark scores drive model selection, resource allocation, and research priorities for large language models, yet training data contamination is pervasive: trillion-token web crawls routinely ingest benchmark items \\citep{oren2024proving}, and practitioners cannot determine how much of a reported score reflects genuine capability versus memorization \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu,chang2025benchmarking}. The community has responded with twenty mitigation strategies \\citep{sun2025emperor}, contamination-free benchmarks \\citep{white2024livebench}, and dynamic regeneration \\citep{dong_2024_cdd}, yet a fundamental question remains unanswered: what is the actual effect size of contamination on aggregate scores? Without this measurement, every mitigation decision is made in the dark. Quantifying the real effect of contamination on scores is therefore a prerequisite for any calibration decision.\n\nThe literature implicitly assumes that the effect is large. Mitigation papers develop elaborate countermeasures; replacement benchmarks sacrifice comparability with established evaluations; correction methods like DCR \\citep{xu2025dcr} apply aggressive adjustments. Yet no study has directly measured the aggregate effect size under controlled conditions with uncertainty quantification. The absence of this measurement leaves the field unable to distinguish settings where contamination genuinely distorts rankings from those where it is detectable but practically harmless.\n\nDetection methods such as Min-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang_2024_minkpp}, ConStat \\citep{dekoninck2024constat}, and Kernel Divergence Signatures \\citep{choi_2025_kds} can flag contaminated items but stop at binary classification. They do not quantify how much scores are inflated or produce adjusted scores with uncertainty estimates. IRT-based evaluation frameworks \\citep{polo_2024_tinybenchmarks,habba_2025_growing_pains} apply psychometric ability estimation to improve evaluation efficiency, but treat all items as equally trustworthy and ignore contamination entirely \\citep{lalor_2024_irt_nlp}. \\citet{han2026quantifying} apply psychometric DIF to quantify contamination effects on item parameters. Our work differs along three axes: (a)~we construct a controlled testbed with known ground-truth contamination labels, whereas they analyze naturally contaminated models where true contamination status is unknown; (b)~we derive identifiability thresholds ($J \\geq 50$, AUC $\\geq 0.55$) that provide operational guidelines for when $\\gamma$-based diagnosis becomes reliable; and (c)~we characterize signal failure modes, including a directional reversal in Min-K\\%++ at the 0.5B scale, that affect detection pipeline design. No existing method provides an end-to-end framework for measuring contamination effect size with uncertainty quantification on existing benchmarks.\n\nThis paper asks: does calibrating contaminated scores require accurate per-item detection, or can a principled measurement model separate ability from noise more generally? We develop ContamCal to investigate this question. Stage~1 fuses multiple detection signals into continuous per-item exposure scores via training-free Reciprocal Rank Fusion \\citep{cormack_2009_rrf}; this sidesteps the meta-circularity of learned aggregators that would require the very contamination labels we lack. Stage~2 embeds these exposure scores as covariates in a contamination-augmented IRT model (2PL+$\\gamma$) that jointly estimates latent ability $\\theta$ and a domain-level contamination effect $\\gamma_d$. Our experiments span four independent model families from four major labs (Alibaba, Microsoft, Allen AI, Google), trained on independent corpora with distinct pre-training recipes, covering 0.5B--14.7B parameters. Under SFT injection across this range, contamination shifts aggregate accuracy by only ${\\sim}$0.6--0.7 percentage points at $\\leq$3B, smaller than the estimation noise of any correction method (pretraining-phase contamination, which may produce larger effects \\citep{schaeffer2026causally}, remains an open question). Raw accuracy is already well-calibrated; neither IRT nor $\\gamma$-based correction improves upon it. The $\\gamma$ parameter nonetheless provides diagnostic value: it reveals domain-level heterogeneity within a model family (Social Sciences $\\gamma_d = -0.63$, posterior $P(\\gamma_d\\!<\\!0) = 99.7\\%$), while the decomposition analysis explains \\textit{why} aggregate scores remain robust despite item-level contamination.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Identifiability analysis with a null testbed result.} Simulation studies establish that reliable $\\gamma$ recovery requires $J \\geq 50$ at signal AUC $\\geq 0.55$ (posterior CI width $\\propto J^{-0.48}$), well above current LLM evaluation practice ($J = 8$--$30$). These thresholds delineate when contamination-augmented IRT transitions from exploratory to confirmatory. As a testbed result across four independent model families (0.5B--14.7B), SFT injection shifts aggregate accuracy by only ${\\sim}$0.6--0.7pp at $\\leq$3B, smaller than the estimation noise of any correction method.\n    \\item \\textbf{Enabling analysis and practical signal findings.} RRF-guided item filtering is a prerequisite for IRT convergence: single-signal selection compresses exposure variance and causes convergence failure ($\\hat{R} = 1.53$, ESS$=$7), while RRF fusion preserves broader distributions necessary for $\\gamma$ identifiability ($\\Delta\\tau = 0.18$ vs.\\ size-matched random, $0.27$ vs.\\ full 2000-item random). As a standalone practical finding, Min-K\\%++ exhibits directional reversal at the 0.5B scale (Cohen's $d = -1.55$ to $-1.98$), where the signal is actively misleading rather than merely ineffective.\n\\end{enumerate}\n\nAdditionally, within-family domain-level analysis reveals directional heterogeneity (Social Sciences $P(\\gamma_d\\!<\\!0) = 99.7\\%$, surviving Bonferroni correction for eight tests), a suggestive finding that requires higher-powered replication with $J \\geq 50$ models.",
        "numLines": 17,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 17
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/introduction.tex"
  },
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "d3bb3129-dc6e-46ff-8279-297dee511e93",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/projects/contamcal-irt-calibration/docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/projects/contamcal-irt-calibration/docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experimental Setup}\n\\label{sec:experiments}\n\n\\subsection{Controlled SFT Injection}\n\\label{sec:injection}\n\nWe construct a controlled contamination testbed spanning four independent model families from four labs, trained on independent corpora with distinct pre-training recipes: Qwen2.5 \\citep{qwen2024qwen25} (0.5B, 1.5B, 3B, 7B, 14B), Phi-4 \\citep{abdin2024phi4} (14.7B), OLMo-2 \\citep{olmo2team2025} (7B), and Gemma-2 \\citep{gemmateam2024gemma2} (9B). All models are fine-tuned with a unified LoRA configuration \\citep{hu2022lora} ($r=16$, $\\alpha=32$, lr$=5\\times10^{-5}$, bf16) on a general-purpose SFT corpus into which benchmark items are injected at five dosage levels: d000 (0\\%, clean control), d005 (5\\%), d025 (25\\%), d050 (50\\%), and d100 (100\\%). Contaminated items augment (not replace) the base SFT corpus; at d100, 16{,}533 benchmark items are added beyond 50{,}000 base examples.\n\nQwen2.5 serves as the primary within-family series, providing five scale points (0.5B--14B) for studying scale-dependent contamination effects. Models at $\\leq$3B are trained with two seeds (s42, s123); 7B and 14B use two and one seed respectively. The three cross-family models (Phi-4, OLMo-2, Gemma-2) are each trained at five dosages with a single seed. This yields 24 within-family checkpoints at $\\leq$3B (existing), 15 additional Qwen models at 7B--14B, and 15 cross-family models, for a total of 54 model checkpoints.\n\nWe exclude 0.5B models from MMLU and ARC IRT due to answer-position bias that correlates with dosage (A-option rate: 46\\% at d000 to 98\\% at d100; \\S\\ref{sec:signal_failure}). Three initial 3B checkpoints trained with inconsistent LoRA hyperparameters were retrained under the unified protocol (Appendix~\\ref{sec:exclusion_details}).\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Family Validation}\n\\label{sec:cross_family}\n\nThe cross-family design tests whether contamination effects generalize beyond a single model family. Phi-4 (14.7B) provides a second data point at the 14B frontier from an independent lab; OLMo-2 (7B) and Gemma-2 (9B) validate at the 7--9B band. Each cross-family model is trained at all five dosage levels with a single seed. This single-seed design provides limited statistical power per family; cross-family results should be interpreted as consistency checks rather than independent confirmatory analyses.\n\n\\subsection{Benchmarks, Domains, and Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:benchmarks}\n\nWe evaluate on three benchmarks spanning distinct cognitive demands and response formats.\n\n\\textbf{MMLU} \\citep{hendrycks2021mmlu}: 14,042 multiple-choice items across 57 subjects, grouped into 8 cognitive domains (Math, Science, CS \\& Engineering, Medical, Humanities, Social Sciences, Business \\& Law, Other). This stratification reveals within-benchmark heterogeneity that aggregate scoring obscures (\\S\\ref{sec:results}).\n\n\\textbf{ARC-Challenge} \\citep{clark2018arc}: 1,172 multiple-choice science reasoning items.\n\n\\textbf{GSM8K} \\citep{cobbe2021gsm8k}: 1,319 open-ended math problems whose free-form generation format avoids multiple-choice artifacts.\n\nWe assess calibration quality with six metrics: (1)~mean absolute error (MAE) between calibrated and ground-truth clean accuracy; (2)~Kendall $\\tau$ for rank preservation; (3)~credible interval coverage at 50\\%, 80\\%, and 95\\% levels; (4)~posterior predictive $p$-value (PPP $\\in [0.1, 0.9]$ indicates acceptable fit); (5)~$\\gamma$ direction: posterior probability $P(\\gamma\\!<\\!0 \\mid \\text{data})$, which reflects evidence strength for a contamination effect; and (6)~split-$\\hat{R}$ convergence ($<1.01$).\n\n\\subsection{Baselines}\n\\label{sec:baselines}\n\nWe compare against seven baselines: \\textbf{B0-Raw} (no adjustment), \\textbf{B1-Remove\\&Rescore} (drop flagged items; B1b uses RRF-filtered items), \\textbf{B2-Single2PL+$\\gamma$} (aggregate $\\gamma$ without domain stratification), \\textbf{B3-Vanilla2PL} ($\\gamma=0$, isolating IRT ability estimation), \\textbf{B4-LinearRegression} (residuals on exposure scores), \\textbf{B5-Emperor} (mitigation strategies from \\citet{sun2025emperor}; B5b uses the best-performing variant), and \\textbf{B6-DCR} (our reimplementation following \\citealp{xu2025dcr}).",
        "numLines": 34,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 34
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex"
  },
```

```json
{
  "text": "[severity: critical] Paper Agent scope expansion patch 审查（≤14.7B + 4 families）\n\n## 审查结论：3 个 critical + 1 个 warning，需修复后再定稿\n\n### CRITICAL\n\n**C1. Abstract 未同步更新**\nabstract.tex 仍写 \"Qwen2.5 (0.5B--3B)\"，未反映 scope expansion 到 4 families / 14.7B。Abstract 是审稿人第一接触点，与正文 §4.1-4.2 的 4-family scope 严重不一致。必须同步更新。\n\n**C2. Contribution 1 claim-evidence 不匹配**\nIntroduction 第 13 行：\"across four independent model families (0.5B--14.7B), SFT injection shifts aggregate accuracy by only ~0.6--0.7pp at ≤3B\"。问题：\n- \"0.6--0.7pp at ≤3B\" 数据来源仅为 Qwen2.5 within-family，不是 four families\n- 14B 实测 d000→d100 = +1.92pp，d000→d050 = +2.28pp，远超 0.6-0.7pp\n- 将 Qwen-only ≤3B 结论与 \"four independent model families\" 并列，审稿人会直接质疑 cherry-picking\n- 修复方向：明确区分 ≤3B（Qwen within-family）和 7B+（跨家族）的效应量，或等跨家族 eval 数据到齐后统一更新数字\n\n**C3. Introduction 预设 Variant A 叙事**\nIntroduction 第 9 行：\"Raw accuracy is already well-calibrated; neither IRT nor γ-based correction improves upon it.\" 这预设了 Variant A（contamination effect negligible）。但 14B 结果处于 gray zone（1.92-2.28pp），7B d050/d100 eval 尚未完成，cross-family eval 全部未开始。我此前的独立评估已建议推迟 A/B 决策。在数据未齐时锁定 Variant A 叙事是重大风险——若后续数据倾向 Variant B，全文需大改。\n\n### WARNING\n\n**W1. \"54 total models\" 算术待澄清**\n§4.1 声称 \"24 within-family checkpoints at ≤3B\"，但 Qwen ≤3B = 3 scales × 5 dosages × 2 seeds = 30。排除 0.5B（因 position bias）后 = 2 scales × 5 dosages × 2 seeds = 20。\"24\" 的来源不清楚，需要在文中明确计算方式或修正数字。\n\n### PASS\n\n- D144 framing 执行正确：4 labs + independent corpora，无 \"architecture lineage\" / \"scaling law\" 违规 ✓\n- 新增 3 条引用（abdin2024phi4, olmo2team2025, gemmateam2024gemma2）arXiv ID 时间合理 ✓\n- AI 写作模式检查通过：无 em-dash、无 -ing phrase 结尾、无黑名单词 ✓\n- §4.2 诚实承认 single-seed 限制 ✓\n- limitations.tex scale ceiling 已更新至 14.7B ✓\n\n### 建议\n\n1. 冻结 Abstract 和 Introduction 中的定量 claim，等 7B d050/d100 + 跨家族 eval 全部完成后，根据完整数据一次性更新\n2. 当前 Introduction 可先用占位表述如 \"SFT injection shifts aggregate accuracy by modest amounts that vary with scale\"，避免锁定具体数字\n3. \"54 models\" 需附简明算术（如脚注），审稿人会验算",
```
